The only major program offering free stopovers on one-way partner awards

## Flying Business Class Across Two Continents for the Price of One Imagine settling into a fully flat bed on a Boeing 787-9 somewhere over the Pacific, a warm towel in your hand, Japan behind you and Singapore ahead. You've already spent three days eating ramen in Tokyo — a stopover you added to your award ticket for a mere 5,000 extra points. This isn't a once-in-a-lifetime splurge. This is what the ChooseFI community calls a **nearly free** trip, engineered through one of the most underappreciated sweet spots in the entire travel rewards ecosystem. The Air Canada Aeroplan stopover rule is genuinely one of the last great loopholes in loyalty travel. Here's the math that makes your jaw drop: a business class flight from Toronto to Singapore — routed through Tokyo with a multi-day stopover — retails for **$8,000 to $10,000 in cash**. You can book that same itinerary for **70,000 Aeroplan points** (plus 5,000 for the stopover, so 75,000 total). At a conservative cash value of $8,000, you're extracting **10.7 cents per point** — more than double what most programs offer, and squarely within Aeroplan's documented range of **5.7 to 14.3 cents per point**. To put that in FI terms: if you value your points at even the low end of that range — 5.7 cents per point — 75,000 Aeroplan points are worth **$4,275 in travel**. At the high end, you're looking at **$10,725**. That's a portfolio-level return on the credit card spending and transfer partners you've already been accumulating. What makes this feel different from other premium redemptions is the *shape* of the trip itself. You're not just flying business class — you're building a mini round-the-world itinerary. Tokyo and Singapore in the same award ticket. Two of the world's great cities. Two business class flights. One redemption. The inefficiency here isn't accidental — it's baked into how Aeroplan prices stopovers at a flat 5,000 miles, regardless of where you stop. The program is essentially inviting you to stack experiences on a single award, and the ChooseFI community is the group paying close enough attention to accept that invitation.

The core insight is this: Air Canada Aeroplan uses a fixed partner award chart for Star Alliance flights, and it prices stopovers at a flat 5,000-mile premium regardless of routing complexity. That single policy decision transforms a standard business class award into a multi-destination adventure at a fraction of the combined retail cost.

Here's the mechanism. When Aeroplan relaunched in 2020, it did something most programs had abandoned years earlier: it kept a fixed-rate award chart for partner airlines. While programs like United MileagePlus moved to fully dynamic pricing — where award costs fluctuate with demand — Aeroplan locked in predictable rates for Star Alliance partners. The YYZ to SIN corridor in business class sits at a rate that reflects the geography, not the current demand spike or revenue management algorithm. That's the pricing inefficiency the ChooseFI community can systematically exploit.

Layer on top of that the stopover rule: Aeroplan allows one free stopover on one-way partner awards for just 5,000 additional miles. A stopover is defined as a connection of more than 24 hours. So your Toronto → Tokyo → Singapore routing becomes a Toronto → Tokyo (stopover 3 days) → Singapore itinerary, all on one award ticket, for 75,000 points total. You've essentially purchased two separate international business class flights for the price of one, plus a nominal surcharge.

The other critical piece: Aeroplan charges zero fuel surcharges on partner awards. This was a deliberate policy decision in the 2020 relaunch and it's a massive advantage. Programs like British Airways Avios and Singapore KrisFlyer can add $500–$1,500 in surcharges to long-haul business class awards. With Aeroplan, you pay government taxes only — typically $50–$150 on most itineraries.

You don't need to fly Air Canada or hold an Air Canada credit card to access this. Aeroplan accepts transfers from several major flexible currency programs at a 1:1 ratio:

Chase Ultimate Rewards is your best friend here because transfers are instant, which matters when you're racing to book a specific award seat before someone else claims it. If you hold a Chase Sapphire Preferred, Chase Sapphire Reserve, or Ink Business Preferred, your UR points can become Aeroplan points in seconds.

The booking window is 355 days out, which gives you nearly a full year of planning runway — a significant advantage for families coordinating schedules or travelers targeting peak periods in Tokyo or Singapore. Partner award availability on Aeroplan is generally solid for the Star Alliance network, with business class seats on ANA, Japan Airlines (where applicable), and Singapore Airlines routes opening up regularly. Availability scores a 4 out of 5 for this sweet spot, which reflects genuine accessibility rather than theoretical possibility.

Free calculator shows when you could reach financial independence — plus a savings rate tracker, expense audit, and more.

Never transfer points until you have confirmed, bookable award space in hand. Go to aeroplan.com and use the flight search tool. Select "Business" class, enter your origin and destination (e.g., Toronto YYZ to Singapore SIN), and search for one-way awards. Aeroplan's search engine will show you available partner space directly — you don't need a separate search on partner airline websites, though checking ana.co.jp or singaporeair.com for business class availability can help you triangulate what's open before logging into Aeroplan.

Look for awards showing Star Alliance partner carriers (ANA, Singapore Airlines, and others depending on routing). The system will display the points cost and any routing options. Aeroplan's booking window opens 355 days in advance, so set a calendar reminder to search the moment your target travel window becomes available.

Once you've found an outbound flight you like (e.g., Toronto → Tokyo), call Aeroplan at 1-800-361-5373 or use the online multi-city search to add a stopover at your midpoint city. A stopover is any connection exceeding 24 hours. The system will add 5,000 points to your base award cost. Confirm the full itinerary shows both the Tokyo stopover segment and the onward Tokyo → Singapore flight in the cabin class you want.

Note: Online booking for complex multi-city awards with stopovers sometimes requires a phone call to complete. Budget 30–45 minutes for the call and have your routing clearly written out before you dial.

Determine which flexible currency you'll use. If you have Chase Ultimate Rewards, transfer to Aeroplan first — it's instant. Log into your Chase account, navigate to "Transfer to Travel Partners," select Air Canada Aeroplan, and complete the transfer. Points appear in your Aeroplan account immediately.

If using Amex Membership Rewards, Capital One, or Bilt, initiate the transfer and expect a 1–2 day processing window. Have your Aeroplan member number ready. Do NOT transfer until you've confirmed availability in Step 1.

Once your points land in your Aeroplan account, return to aeroplan.com and complete the booking within the same browser session if possible. Pay the government taxes and fees with a credit card that earns rewards on travel purchases. Taxes on a routing like YYZ → NRT → SIN are typically in the $100–$200 range — no fuel surcharges.

After booking, download or screenshot your itinerary confirmation. Verify the cabin class on each segment. Check in on the Aeroplan website 24 hours before each flight and confirm your seat assignments. For the stopover city, you are responsible for your own accommodation — budget accordingly, and consider this the best "forced layover" you'll ever take.

A stopover is a deliberate break in your journey at a connecting city, where you stay for more than 24 hours before continuing to your final destination. Most loyalty programs treat stopovers as separate tickets, requiring you to pay miles for each segment independently. Aeroplan breaks this convention by allowing one free stopover on one-way awards, meaning your connecting city becomes a bonus destination at zero additional mileage cost. For example, a flight from New York to Tokyo with a 5-day stopover in Istanbul costs the same 75,000 Aeroplan points as flying directly — but you get to explore Istanbul for nearly a week as part of the trip. The stopover must be at a connection point that makes geographic sense for your routing, but with 26+ Star Alliance airlines and hundreds of hub cities, the options are vast.

Let's build out a specific redemption so the math becomes undeniable.

The Itinerary: You depart Toronto Pearson (YYZ) on an ANA Boeing 787-9 bound for Tokyo Narita (NRT). You've built in a 3-day stopover in Tokyo — enough time for a morning at Tsukiji, an evening in Shinjuku, and a day trip to Nikko. Then you board a Singapore Airlines flight from NRT to Singapore Changi (SIN) in Business Class.

The Cash Price: A business class itinerary covering this exact routing — YYZ to NRT to SIN — retails in the range of $8,000 to $10,000, depending on season and carrier. Booking two separate one-way tickets (YYZ-NRT and NRT-SIN) in business class would easily exceed that, often landing closer to $10,000–$12,000 combined.

The Points Cost: 70,000 Aeroplan points for the base award + 5,000 for the stopover = 75,000 points total, plus approximately $120–$180 in government taxes. No fuel surcharges.

The CPP Calculation: At a midpoint cash value of $9,000, your 75,000 points are generating 12 cents per point. Even at the conservative $8,000 end, you're at 10.7 cents per point — well within the documented 5.7–14.3 cent range and significantly above the ChooseFI rule of thumb of 2 cents per point for most redemptions.

On the ANA 787-9 YYZ–NRT segment, you're in ANA's Business Staggered seat — a fully flat bed with direct aisle access, Japanese hospitality, and one of the best business class dining experiences in the sky. The Tokyo stopover is yours to explore however you like. Then Singapore Airlines' Business Class on the NRT–SIN leg offers its own benchmark product — a refined seat, excellent service, and arrival into Changi, consistently ranked the world's best airport.

You've visited two of Asia's most celebrated cities, flown on two world-class airlines, slept flat across the Pacific, and your out-of-pocket cash cost was under $200. That is the ChooseFI travel rewards mindset in its purest form.

Tokyo in cherry blossom season (late March to early April) and autumn foliage season (November) books up incredibly fast — even in business class. Set a calendar reminder for exactly 355 days before your target departure and search the moment Aeroplan's window opens. Partner award space on popular routes gets claimed quickly.

Because Chase Ultimate Rewards transfers to Aeroplan instantly, you can hold an award seat without pre-positioning points in your account. Find the space first, then transfer exactly what you need. This is a significant tactical advantage over programs that require 2–5 day transfer windows.

Aeroplan allows open jaw routing. You can fly INTO Tokyo and OUT of Singapore on separate awards, pairing this business class redemption with a separate economy or business award home. Build your Southeast Asia trip so you land in one city and depart from another — no backtracking, better geography, same award pricing framework.

This is the single most common mistake in all of travel rewards. If you transfer 75,000 Chase UR to Aeroplan and then discover the award space disappeared, you cannot transfer back. Chase points are gone. Confirm bookable space first, transfer second, book third.

Marriott transfers to Aeroplan at a 3:1 ratio — meaning you need 225,000 Marriott points to generate 75,000 Aeroplan points. That's an extraordinarily poor ratio for this redemption. Use Chase, Amex, Capital One, or Bilt instead.

The Chase Sapphire Reserve earns 3x on travel and dining. The Amex Platinum earns 5x on flights booked directly. Build your Aeroplan balance through the transfer partners rather than flying Air Canada itself — the flexibility is far more valuable.

Unlike many programs that expire your miles after 12–18 months of inactivity, Aeroplan points never expire. This means you can accumulate slowly and deliberately — a natural fit for the FI community's patient, systematic approach to building travel rewards balances.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

This redemption is ideal for couples and solo travelers with a genuine interest in Asia — specifically anyone who has ever dreamed of Japan and Southeast Asia in the same trip without paying for two separate premium tickets. If your travel bucket list includes Tokyo and Singapore in the same itinerary, this is the single most efficient way to get there in business class.

Minimum points needed: ~75,000 Aeroplan points (70,000 base + 5,000 stopover). Build this through Chase UR, Amex MR, Capital One, or Bilt transfers.

For the FI-minded traveler, this is a high-ROI, low-cash-cost adventure that fits perfectly into a strategy of saving aggressively while experiencing the world nearly free.

## Why This Is Rated Moderate — And How to Handle It This sweet spot earns a **moderate difficulty** rating — not because the math is complicated, but because the booking process has more moving parts than a simple domestic award. **What makes it moderate:** 1. **Complex routing with a stopover** — Aeroplan's online tool doesn't always handle multi-city stopover bookings cleanly. You may need to call Aeroplan to finalize the itinerary, which adds a step and requires you to have your routing clearly planned before you dial. 2. **Partner award availability** — You're booking seats on ANA, Singapore Airlines, or other Star Alliance carriers, not Air Canada. Partner space is real and accessible (this scores a 4/5 for availability), but it requires searching specifically for it on aeroplan.com and sometimes triangulating with partner airline websites. 3. **Points transfer timing** — You need points in your Aeroplan account before completing the booking, which means coordinating a transfer after confirming space exists. Chase's instant transfer largely solves this problem. **Three tips to make it manageable:** - **Use aeroplan.com's multi-city search first** to understand what routing options exist before calling. Come to any phone call with a primary and backup itinerary already researched. - **Book 6–12 months out** using the 355-day window. More availability, more flexibility, less stress. - **Do one transfer before you need to** — move a small amount of points to Aeroplan just to practice the process and verify your accounts are linked. If you've successfully booked a domestic business class award before, this is a natural next step. The complexity is manageable, and the reward — business class across two ocean crossings with a Tokyo stopover — is absolutely worth the extra 45 minutes of work.

Identify your final destination and choose a stopover city that lies along a logical routing. Popular stopover cities include Istanbul (Turkish Airlines hub), Singapore (Singapore Airlines hub), Tokyo (ANA hub), Frankfurt (Lufthansa hub), and Zurich (Swiss hub). The stopover city must be a connection point that Aeroplan considers valid for your route.

On aircanada.com, use the multi-city search option. Enter your origin to the stopover city as the first segment, and the stopover city to your final destination as the second segment. Set business class and search. The system will show you combined availability and pricing with the stopover included.

Once you confirm availability, transfer the required points from Chase Ultimate Rewards (instant to 24 hours), Amex Membership Rewards (1-3 days), Capital One (1-2 days), or Bilt (1-2 days). Transfer only after confirming both segments have availability.

Follow the booking flow on aircanada.com, selecting both segments. The system will recognize the stopover and price it as a single one-way award. Confirm the total mileage cost matches the one-way rate for your destination — the stopover should add zero additional miles.

With up to 45 days at your stopover city, plan accommodations and activities. Consider using hotel points for your stopover stay — Hyatt points in Istanbul or IHG points in Singapore pair well with an Aeroplan stopover. The stopover is the bonus trip, so treat it as a mini-vacation within your larger journey.

Aeroplan allows stopovers of up to 45 days on one-way awards. This is more than enough time for a genuine visit to your stopover city. You can stay as briefly as 24 hours (anything less is considered a connection, not a stopover) or as long as 45 days before continuing to your final destination.

No. The free stopover on a one-way award is included at no additional mileage cost. Your total point cost is determined by the origin-to-final-destination pricing, not by the number of stops along the way. A flight from New York to Bangkok with a stopover in Istanbul costs the same as New York to Bangkok direct.

Aeroplan prices round trips as two one-way tickets. Each one-way award allows one free stopover, so a round-trip booking can include up to two stopovers — one on the outbound and one on the return. This effectively turns a round trip into a four-destination itinerary.

Lufthansa, Swiss, Austrian, and THAI Airways impose significant fuel surcharges on award tickets that can add $100 to $400 per segment. To minimize out-of-pocket costs, route through carriers with no or low surcharges: Turkish Airlines, ANA, EVA Air, Singapore Airlines (on most routes), and United. Check the fare breakdown before confirming your booking.

Aeroplan's free stopover on one-way awards is a genuinely unique feature that no other major North American loyalty program offers. The ability to spend a week in Istanbul, Singapore, or Tokyo as a free add-on to your business class award makes Aeroplan one of the most valuable programs in the travel rewards ecosystem. Combine this with access to 26+ Star Alliance carriers, online booking for stopover routings, and 1:1 transfers from Chase, Amex, Capital One, and Bilt, and you have a program that delivers outsized value for the ChooseFI community. Build a transferable points balance, identify your dream two-destination trip, and let Aeroplan's stopover rule turn one award into two vacations.

CM: Dalot could reunite with Amorim at Milan as Man Utd deal winds down

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Diogo Dalot has once again been linked with a potential return to AC Milan, where he spent the 2020-21 season on loan.

Calciomercato.com report that Roma’s visit to face Manchester United at Old Trafford will not only be an opportunity for the Giallorossi to face and perhaps erase the ghosts of the past, but also to watch someone up close who could be a name for the not-too-distant future: Diogo Dalot.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The Portuguese full-back’s contract is expiring at the end of next season and although he is happy in Manchester, many clubs are beginning to ask his entourage for details.

Roma were one step away from landing the defender in the summer of 2021 when the Portuguese coach José Mourinho was on the bench. The negotiations dragged on for a long time, but in the end it didn’t go through.

The reason ended up being quite simple: the Red Devils never agreed to a loan with option to buy formula while Roma – who were still severely limited by Financial Fair Play limitations – could not offer anything more.

Dalot knows Italy and Serie A well, and he recently spoke about his loan experience with Milan in an interview with The Player’s Tribune.

“It was one of the most important years of my career. I remember the way the club welcomed me and how welcome I felt in a year that was important to me, one in which I needed to regain my confidence in my football. Milan helped me get back to those levels,” he said.

“Every day, Paolo Maldini was on the sidelines to watch us train. Rain, shine, or cold, he was always there, in a suit and tie. He would say to me: ‘Mark like this’ or ‘Tackle it.’ Other times, he would scold me for trying too hard. ‘You’re an extraordinary player, don’t worry, you’ll see, everything will be fine’.”

As mentioned, Dalot’s deal expires in 2028 but there is the presence of a one-year clause that United can trigger. There are a number of teams monitoring his situation including Inter, Milan and Juventus, who have all enquired in recent weeks.

Nothing concrete has emerged yet, as Dalot happy in Manchester and isn’t thinking about the future. However, if things don’t change, a departure – perhaps even for a low-cost fee – could become a realistic possibility.

With the Rossoneri needing defenders, could they try pounce on a reunion for a versatile player that they and Ruben Amorim know well?

American Airlines Passenger Stuck Overnight Couldn’t Get A Hotel Voucher—They Got A Credit Card Pitch Instead

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

An American Airlines passenger says they couldn’t get a hotel voucher during a flight delay. The chat agent did, however, have something else ready: a pitch to open a Citi credit card, spend $2,500, and earn 50,000 miles. That’s not even as good as the standard offer! American’s stated purpose is “To Care for People on Life’s Journey.” That journey includes a sales presentation while you’re trying to figure out where to sleep. The passenger shared a screenshot of the exchange. It begins with the agent asking, “Does any of that work for you?” Then comes a lengthy offer for the Citi / AAdvantage Platinum Select Mastercard: the initial bonus, free checked bags, double miles at restaurants and gas stations, and a request for permission to send a one-time email with more information. American Airlines offered me a credit card deal during a conversation about a delayed flight and their inability to provide a hotel voucher from mildlyinfuriating Always be selling! Always be closing! It’s Glengarry Glen Ross, and the passenger asking for help is the lead. American Pays Agents To Make These Pitches In May 2023, I reported that American’s reservations agents would switch from pitching Avis rental cars to Citi credit cards. At the time, I argued that agents should judge whether the conversation had gone well enough for a customer to be receptive. American’s AAdvantage Cash support materials explain why an agent might make the ask either way: Contact center and Premium Guest Services representatives earn at least $50 for each approved application carrying their referral code. That rises to $100 as they move through annual sales tiers. There are also $100, $300, and $500 bonuses for reaching Gold, Platinum, and Diamond. They earn elite status like we do. There’s also a separate guide to pitching cards during chats, complete with prescribed transitions from talking about travel benefits to offering the card. American Already Knows When To Stop Selling I’ve written for years about credit card announcements that interrupt sleep on early morning flights. As Kevin Williamson once put it, [T]here is the Clockwork Orange sensation of being literally strapped into a seat while someone screams corporate banalities at you on a loudspeaker fifteen inches from your head… It is a “limited-time” offer in the sense that the sun eventually will run out of gas and become a dying star, first engulfing the Earth in fire and then leaving the wreck of the solar system a sterile plane of interstellar cold and utter silence, which will be interrupted only by some addled flight attendant screeching about the limited-time Barclays card offer. The airline’s guidelines at least suggest: Use the approved script, which should take about a minute. Make the announcement only once per flight segment, at cruise before descent, between 8 a.m. and 9 p.m. Skip late night and redeye flights. Refrain from offering the card or making announcements during delays, cancellations, diversions, and other events affecting the customer experience. Meanwhile, the pressure to sell keeps growing. On October 15, the flight attendants union objected to American inserting a 15-minute card-sales module into mandatory fourth quarter training. Selling remains voluntary, but management made the training required and offered a $25 qualification reward. They Have A Sales Script For Your Bag Fee, Too American’s airport agent talking points for “Real Time Benefits” identify two moments to pitch: when a customer is about to pay a checked bag fee, and when they’re worried there won’t be overhead bin space for their carry-on. For most instantly approved applicants, the card’s first checked bag benefit and Group 5 boarding can apply to the trip a passenger is already taking. The airline says the boarding group can take roughly seven minutes to update on a digital boarding pass. If the customer has already prepaid for bags, agents are told to shift the pitch to the miles, boarding, and free bags on a future eligible trip. I’ve even proposed plastering aircraft lavatories with credit card ads if attaching revenue to that space would persuade American to give us larger bathrooms since the space becomes monetized rather than deadweight loss. The inflight sales operation has instructions for distributing the commissions fairly among participating flight attendants. Brochures are prefilled with employee numbers, then stacked in a 1–2–3–3–2–1 sequence when three crew members participate. They’ve worked out whose referral code the next passenger gets. A Few Applications Don’t Prove This Is A Good Idea Maybe the number of applications generated from unhappy customers isn’t zero. The agent already has their attention, the sales text is ready to paste, and a small number of approvals can produce real money. But a positive response rate doesn’t establish that the pitch was worth making. You have to count the customers you make angrier, the business they take elsewhere, and whether someone who applies will actually keep and use the card. As I covered in looking at American’s Citi economics, their ambitions depend on acquiring customers, retaining them, and getting their spending. American’s deal announcement projected annual payments approaching $10 billion. They’ve finally come to realize that it matters whether customers like the airline enough to carry it in their wallet. In August, I covered their push for friendlier inflight service to increase card applications. Taking care of passengers makes them more receptive to the pitch. American’s stated purpose is “To Care for People on Life’s Journey.” That journey includes a sales presentation while you’re trying to figure out where to sleep.

The passenger shared a screenshot of the exchange. It begins with the agent asking, “Does any of that work for you?” Then comes a lengthy offer for the Citi / AAdvantage Platinum Select Mastercard: the initial bonus, free checked bags, double miles at restaurants and gas stations, and a request for permission to send a one-time email with more information.

Always be selling! Always be closing! It’s Glengarry Glen Ross, and the passenger asking for help is the lead.

In May 2023, I reported that American’s reservations agents would switch from pitching Avis rental cars to Citi credit cards. At the time, I argued that agents should judge whether the conversation had gone well enough for a customer to be receptive. American’s AAdvantage Cash support materials explain why an agent might make the ask either way: Contact center and Premium Guest Services representatives earn at least $50 for each approved application carrying their referral code. That rises to $100 as they move through annual sales tiers. There are also $100, $300, and $500 bonuses for reaching Gold, Platinum, and Diamond. They earn elite status like we do. There’s also a separate guide to pitching cards during chats, complete with prescribed transitions from talking about travel benefits to offering the card. American Already Knows When To Stop Selling I’ve written for years about credit card announcements that interrupt sleep on early morning flights. As Kevin Williamson once put it, [T]here is the Clockwork Orange sensation of being literally strapped into a seat while someone screams corporate banalities at you on a loudspeaker fifteen inches from your head… It is a “limited-time” offer in the sense that the sun eventually will run out of gas and become a dying star, first engulfing the Earth in fire and then leaving the wreck of the solar system a sterile plane of interstellar cold and utter silence, which will be interrupted only by some addled flight attendant screeching about the limited-time Barclays card offer. The airline’s guidelines at least suggest: Use the approved script, which should take about a minute. Make the announcement only once per flight segment, at cruise before descent, between 8 a.m. and 9 p.m. Skip late night and redeye flights. Refrain from offering the card or making announcements during delays, cancellations, diversions, and other events affecting the customer experience. Meanwhile, the pressure to sell keeps growing. Selling remains voluntary, but management made the training required and offered a $25 qualification reward. 6 Antique Trends Designers Are Watching for 2027

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Above: The kitchen in designer Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat.

Do the words “antique” and “trend” really belong in the same sentence? In many ways, a great antique shop feels like the antidote to trend fatigue: a space where the true must-haves are hidden in dusty corners, with part of the fun in the thrill of the hunt. Yet, as we round out 2026, there’s no denying the current design cycle is casting off perfection for patina. Antiques are en vogue, and top designers are keeping a close eye on the periods, pieces, and yes, trends, that are poised to find new life in 2027.

“The return of interest in antiques reflects a trend towards more personal, unique interiors, and away from strict modern rooms which can often be cool and generic,” says ELLE Decor A-List designer Jamie Drake. This shift paves the way for more playful interactions between pieces, unexpected layering, and unabashed personality.

Other experts second the motion for more character. “I’m seeing clients really drawn to antiques with more to them than meets the eye: pieces that have a bit of humor or whimsy, or pieces with great provenance,” says ELLE Decor Rising Star William Cullum. (He lists a “ridiculously giant butterfly light fixture” from New Orleans, rattan chairs from Bing Crosby’s estate, and a disco ball from Cyndi Lauper’s Bonham’s sale in his list of recent acquisitions—professional and personal.)

Luckily for the amateur antiquer, not all trending vintage styles require a substantial space commitment or glamorous provenance—although an interesting backstory helps. “Antiques are always about a story: where you found them, who owned them, and how that story will continue on after us,” Cullum says.

Ahead, we asked top designers and experts to share the most inspiring antique trends of the moment. From neoclassic mirrors to hand-painted chests paired with modern art, their takes are sure to inform your next estate sale rummage, or inspire you to restyle an old heirloom.

We called mirrored surfaces a major trend to watch for fall 2026, and antique trends have followed suit. Unexpected (or better yet, artfully manipulated) reflections can add rich dimension and movement to any space, but in this category, antiques create a window to the past that’s tough to beat.

“One of the easiest ways to add a sense of history to a room is to include an antique mirror,” says Drake. “Whether you choose a sober and strict neo-classic piece or a more flamboyant baroque one, reflections of the past add depth and sparkle.”

“One trend I’m observing is an uptick in antique dowry chests,” antiques dealer Ali Mahon says. From Pennsylvania Dutch to Swedish Folk and Spanish carved, she’s noticed clients gravitating toward more delicately adorned storage over spare. With thoughtfully mixed furniture (from style to period) becoming more desirable than matching sets, Mahon sees more hand-painted antique surfaces in our future: “With their decorative tulips, leaves, birds, and hearts, dowry chests create a colorful splash in any room.”

ELLE Decor Rising Star Reagan Gail Struble agrees—although delicate designs are not a requirement of the trend, in her book: “Something about a playful wash of color on an awesome old piece of furniture is very alluring, especially when you can see the age and texture of the wood peeking through,” she says. “Hand-painted case pieces add a little punch of charm to any room.”

Luckily, these antiques can be just as functional as they are fashionable. Mahon recommends using a painted chest as a coffee table, at the foot of the bed, or in a space that could benefit from a bit more romance (and a few extra drawers). When styling your find in a modern space, Struble says that contrast is key: try a “chippy Swedish chest of drawers” with an unexpected photograph or painting above it.

According to ELLE Decor A-List designer Nick Olsen, Art Deco pieces are setting the tone for 2027 antique trends—although, if you ask him, they were never out. “I'm particularly inspired by the [art deco influence in the] Gujarat palace in India,” he says. “I'm fascinated by how every element seems to have a wide border: marble floors, carpets, walls of veneer and mohair velvet upholstery.”

According to Olsen, using Art Deco design compositions can do as much for a room as actual pieces from the period. “I like the idea of contrasting borders: solid textures punctuated by smaller graphic prints on furniture and more delicate lighting,” he explains. And while borders are easy to create with paint, paneling, and carpet seaming, Olsen challenges clients to push the Art Deco agenda further by bordering with luxe materials.

Whether you’re new to antiquing or simply trying to add more character to a space, lighting is an excellent place to start. “Lighting is the jewelry of the home, so it’s one of my favorite places to bring in something vintage,” says A-Lister Corey Damen Jenkins. “An Art Deco sconce or an antique brass lantern has a warmth to it that’s hard to find in something brand new.”

Adding to an existing space? Jenkins advises starting small: “Swap one overhead fixture or put a pair of vintage lamps on a console, and you’ll be surprised how much the whole room shifts,” he says. “It’s an easy way to add history without starting over.”

“In light of America’s 250th birthday, Americana in general is in vogue,” says Mahon. Among her clients, the trend has manifested in quilts and textiles, portraits of sea captains, trade signs, and ironstone—culminating in an aesthetic she describes as “elevated country—not corny.”

If you’ve noticed that no particular design period seems to reign supreme among designers’ top antique trend picks, it’s not an accident. Heading into 2027, there’s a strong argument to be made that mixed-period design is more influential than any singular reference.

“I love a room that doesn’t belong to any one decade,” Jenkins says. “A nineteenth-century chest with a 1970s lamp on top, or a Lucite chair pulled up to an antique desk. Those are the pairings that make people stop and look twice.” Drake adds that seemingly out-of-place seating is an easy way to add intrigue. “I love including antique chairs as sculptural notes, contrasted against modern upholstery and tables,” he says.

But when it comes to antiquing, there’s really only so much one can plan for. Letting a great find lead the way often turns out to be a better strategy than arriving to an estate sale with a rigid checklist. The best way to keep up with top designers when it comes to antiquing this year? Find a piece that has something to say, place it where it seems to belong, and then try it where it doesn’t.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

7-Minute Yoga to Practice While Sitting at Your Desk

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Yoga while sitting at your desk can be a simple way to soothe physical tension during the work day. This practice was created by instructor Laia Bové, who has taught students of all levels for more than a decade.

If you spend a lot of time hunched over your desk, you already know that it’s important to break the stagnation of long hours in front of your computer screen. But sometimes the time you think you’ll have to release the tension in your neck, shoulders, and upper back—likely before or after work—disappears due to other responsibilities, distractions, or just plain exhaustion.

This 7-minute yoga practice was made with the idea that you can sneak in a little yoga during work. These stretches target the most common areas where tension and aches arise when sitting at a desk and act as a quick reset. By the end of the seven minutes, you’ll feel a lot looser throughout your upper back and maybe even a little more ready to take on the rest of your day.

You can do this sequence between tasks, meetings, or calls, or after you power off your laptop and before you move on to other activities. You don’t need any special clothes or equipment—in fact, you don’t even need to get up from your chair.

Sit in your desk chair with your feet planted on the floor. Exhale and lower your chin toward your chest. Inhale and lift your chin toward the ceiling. Repeat that a few times.

Next time you exhale and lower your chin toward your chest, keep your chin lowered as you turn your head toward the right, as if you were drawing your nose toward your right shoulder. Take a breath here, then return to center with your chin still lowered. Then draw your nose toward your left shoulder. Return to center, then lift your chin to neutral.

Clasp your hands and rest them lightly behind your head. Point your elbows toward the sides of the room to prevent your hands from placing too much pressure on your head. Inhale as you lift your chest and draw your elbows slightly behind your chest, if possible, and slightly arch your back in a Cow Pose variation.

Keeping your hands where they are, exhale as you round your back, bring your elbows toward each other, and draw your navel toward your spine in a seated variation of Cat Pose. Repeat another round of this seated Cat-Cow variation.

Keeping your hands clasped behind your head, exhale as you lean toward the left, feeling a stretch along your right side. Inhale and return to center. Exhale as you bend toward the right, feeling a stretch along your left side. Inhale and return to center, then release your arms.

Place your fingertips on your shoulders and point your elbows toward the sides of the room. Draw your elbows toward each other in front of your chest as if you want them to touch, then lift them to point toward the ceiling, then point them toward the back wall, and then point them at the floor, moving in a large circular motion. Continue drawing elbow circles as many times as you like. Then switch directions.

Exhale and tilt your head toward the right, drawing your right ear toward your right shoulder. Inhale and return to center. Exhale and tilt your head toward the left, drawing your left ear toward your left shoulder. Repeat a few times.

Reach your arms out to your sides in a T shape, then cross your arms and wrap them around your chest as if you’re giving yourself a big hug. Take a few breaths here in Eagle Arms variation. Then repeat with the opposite arm on top. Release your arms.

With your arms relaxed at your sides, draw your shoulders up toward your ears and squeeze your muscles as tightly as you can—maybe you even scrunch the muscles in your face. Take a big breath in, then release your shoulders down and relax the muscles you were tightening. Repeat one more Shoulder Squeeze.

Take a big breath in through your nose and out through your mouth. Repeat as many times as needed until you’re ready to resume your day.

59-Room Andaz Turks & Caicos At Grace Bay Opening April 2027, Now Bookable

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The much delayed Andaz Turks & Caicos is getting closer to opening. The hotel has now moved forward the date as of which it’s accepting reservations, and we’ve also learned more details about the on-property amenities. Let’s cover all the details, as we’re potentially under five months from the opening…

The Andaz Turks & Caicos at Grace Bay is accepting reservations for stays as of April 1, 2027. This will mark Hyatt’s first hotel in Turks & Caicos, and the first Andaz property in the Caribbean. For that matter, it’s one of the only Andaz properties that’s currently in the pipeline outside of Asia, as the brand has really lost steam.

The 5.5-acre resort will offer four restaurants and bars, three outdoor pools, a spa, a kids club, a fitness center, tennis and pickleball courts, and more. The Andaz will be comprised of 59 rooms and 73 branded residences, so at least the hotel portion of the property will be boutique. It’ll be located directly on Grace Bay, known for offering one of the most beautiful stretches of beach in the Caribbean.

As far as the culinary concepts go, the Andaz Turks & Caicos will feature the following:

Runa Spa & Wellness, the spa at the Andaz, will be around 7,000 square feet. It will have five treatment rooms, along with a sauna, cold plunge, relaxation area, and more.

The opening of this resort has been majorly delayed. It was initially announced in 2018 with a 2021 opening, but the timeline slipped by six years, presumably due to a combination of the pandemic, plus… well, how hotel construction projects often work out. Hopefully there are no additional delays beyond the current planned spring 2027 opening.

With the Andaz Turks & Caicos at Grace Bay bookable, what are rates like? For those redeeming points, this is a Category 8 World of Hyatt property, meaning a free night redemption in a standard room costs 35,000-75,000 points per night.

World of Hyatt’s new award chart sure makes top end redemptions expensive, unfortunately! As of now the hotel is primarily bookable between April and November of 2027, so this doesn’t really cover peak season. For most dates, I currently see rates of either 35,000 points, 45,000 points, or 55,000 points per night. So value here is highly dependent on which of those rates you can find.

When it comes to cash rates, those currently start at around $900-1,300 per night, and that depends on when you’re staying. That’s inclusive of the service charge, but exclusive of the taxes.

If you are looking to book a cash stay here, I’d recommend doing so through the Hyatt Privé program, which offers extra perks like room upgrades, complimentary breakfast, and a hotel credit.

I suppose good options on Grace Bay are limited, and this hotel is boutique. But at these rates, they better offer something significantly better than the typical Andaz experience, in my opinion.

Turks & Caicos is absolutely beautiful, and it’s an easy getaway from the United States, given how close it is to Florida. Historically, the country hasn’t had that many points hotels, though that’s slowly starting to change.

Several years back, we saw the opening of the Ritz-Carlton Turks & Caicos. It has an amazing location on Grace Bay and the grounds of the property are beautiful, but it otherwise doesn’t feel very luxurious, and lacks personality.

So it’ll be nice to have another points property, and I look forward to learning more about the Andaz. Maybe I’m overly optimistic, but I think the Andaz might just end up offering a better experience than the Ritz-Carlton, at least based on the design renderings, and also the more boutique scale of the property. Andaz really is quite an inconsistent brand.

I of course should acknowledge that many people are understandably frustrated by the way that Grace Bay has developed over the years, given all the huge concrete towers that have taken shape along the beach. It has definitely lost its charm a bit, though the beach is still gorgeous.

Even beyond Grace Bay, we’re seeing some other great new options in Turks & Caicos. In 2025, we saw the opening of Salterra Turks & Caicos, a Marriott Luxury Collection property. This is great for those who want a bit more secluded of a trip, since South Caicos Island is a bit less crowded than the area around Providenciales. This is also where Sailrock Resort is located, which is an independent hotel affiliated with Small Luxury Hotels of the World (SLH).

There’s also a Waldorf Astoria Turks & Caicos planned for a 2028 opening, located on Dellis Cay, a nearby island that’s not as developed, which can be reached by a 25-minute boat transfer from Providenciales. That should also be a great option, especially with how the Waldorf Astoria portfolio is developing. However, don’t count on that timeline sticking either.

The Andaz Turks & Caicos is accepting reservations for stays as of April 2027, and it will be located on the famous Grace Bay. The property will have just 59 hotel rooms, in addition to residences, so I like how it’s on a smaller scale than some other properties.

This should be a very nice addition to the Caribbean points hotel market. Hopefully we don’t see any additional delays with the opening…

Rivian spinout Also was working on an e-moto project in Kenya — until it walked away

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Also, the micromobility startup spun out of Rivian in 2025, quietly started and then walked away from an electric two-wheeler pilot project in Kenya earlier this year, as it sought to focus its resources on its consumer e-bike and autonomous vehicle ambitions, TechCrunch has learned.

The project received grant funding from the Shell Foundation, an independent charity established by the British oil and gas giant Shell plc. A section of the Shell Foundation’s website that has since been removed said the project’s goal was to “enable and de-risk global OEM entry into Africa’s EV market by piloting a locally integrated electric two-wheeler platform in Kenya, with potential to scale across East Africa.”

According to the deleted section, Also was expected to work on modular battery designs and distributed EV charging, and planned on “developing a purpose-built electric motorcycle for African riders.”

The opportunity for electric motorcycles seems ripe on the continent, which has a massive two-wheeler market. African mobility company Spiro recently said it has more than 130,000 electric motorcycles operating across seven countries, along with 2,500 battery-swapping stations. This boom has drawn interest from Chinese mobility companies and investors, which are pouring resources into the space.

In a September email, Also told TechCrunch that it had “explored this project” with an unnamed partner, but ultimately “decided to focus on its core roadmap” after completing only “some early-stage work.” Also said the grant funding it received from the Shell Foundation “will follow the project to support its ongoing development,” but declined to offer specifics.

The Shell Foundation told TechCrunch in an email that the project was “intended to generate evidence on driver income uplift potential, operational performance and commercial viability to deepen the company’s understanding of the market opportunity.” But when TechCrunch asked for more specifics, the Shell Foundation declined, citing a nondisclosure agreement with Also. Days later, the project disappeared from the Shell Foundation’s website.

During an interview last month at TechCrunch Disrupt, Also co-founder and president Chris Yu said the Kenya pilot project initially made sense given Also’s mission to build small, purpose-built vehicles that are “first and foremost cost-optimized to do the job that needs to be done in a particular market.”

But other projects took precedence, Yu said, especially the development of small autonomous vehicles — which Also is working on as part of a partnership with DoorDash.

“We piloted and ultimately decided, largely because of just urgency and pace around autonomy, frankly, that our attention would be best, in the near term, served fully focused” on that, he said, adding that he thinks there’s a “high chance that autonomy will show up first, and at a broader scale in these types of purpose-built smaller vehicles than at scale in passenger vehicles.”

Also walked away from the Africa project during a somewhat rocky first year of independence.

Supply chain problems forced the company to repeatedly delay deliveries of its first product, a premium e-bike called the TM-B, frustrating customers who had reserved one. What made matters worse was Also’s relative silence. The company said little for much of 2026 about when the bikes would arrive — a communication failure its leadership now acknowledges was a mistake.

“I think we did a pretty poor job communicating to our reservation holders through the summer when they were all waiting, and we got called out for that,” Yu said in the interview. “But I think it’s actually great. It’s really hard to earn the opportunity for people to care, and to tell us what they think, and to help us improve.”

Getting its first product launch back on track is crucial for Also, which has ambitions far beyond e-bikes. The company plans to sell commercial cargo bikes to companies like Amazon, including four-wheeled models, and has the partnership with DoorDash. These products are next in the company’s pipeline, Yu said, and represent bigger revenue opportunities.

But the TM-B comes first. It’s Also’s most visible product to consumers, and its striking design has drawn plenty of curiosity.

Yu said no single problem was responsible for delaying the bikes, which finally started shipping to customers in early October. Instead, Also spent much of the year playing supply chain Whac-A-Mole, a particularly difficult challenge given the TM-B’s complexity. Yu described the bike as “kind of a computer on wheels.”

At one point, Also found itself competing for the same raw materials used to make printed circuit boards for AI data centers. At other times, the company struggled to navigate certification requirements with different government agencies.

Yu said the scope of these problems, and the pace at which new ones popped up — almost weekly — made it difficult to give customers reliable updates about why their bikes were delayed.

“The entire team is like running at a million miles an hour to get these things produced and shipped to customers, and then it’s also really dynamic as well,” he said. “I think what we could have done better is just [say], straight up: ‘We’re chasing down these things, we’ll have an update shortly.’”

That didn’t happen at first, but Also has steadily improved its communication, earning some goodwill from customers on Reddit. The company will need it as it works through the inevitable kinks that come with a first product. Early customers have already reported problems, including delivery damage and noticeable battery drain.

Former High Museum of Art executive sentenced to 12 months in prison for embezzlement

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Auguste Rodin's The Shade at the High Museum of Art, Atlanta Photo: Marc Merlin, via Flickr

Brady Lum, a former chief operating officer at Atlanta's High Museum of Art, was sentenced to 12 months in federal prison on Monday (2 November) for embezzling more than $600,000 from the institution. The sentencing comes as the High celebrates its centennial and its parent organisation, the Woodruff Arts Center, conducts a major corporate fundraising campaign.

Federal prosecutors said Lum diverted museum funds to pay for personal purchases, including guitars, music lessons and woodworking equipment. He concealed the expenditures through falsified invoices, reimbursement requests and accounting adjustments. The scheme began in September 2019, less than nine months after Lum joined the museum, and continued until its discovery in November 2025.

Lum paid full restitution of $607,688.18 to the High and voluntarily paid approximately $500,000 more to cover costs the museum incurred while uncovering and investigating his criminal activity.

During Monday's hearing, US District judge Michael L. Brown focused extensively on the deterrent purpose of sentencing. Although Brown said he did not believe Lum was likely to reoffend, he emphasised the court's responsibility to discourage similar financial crimes by others in positions of trust.

The judge acknowledged similarities between his own professional and socioeconomic background and Lum's, discussing how sentencing structures have historically been shaped by educated, professionally successful individuals whose circumstances resemble those of many white-collar defendants.

Brown also considered Lum's prompt restitution after the misconduct was discovered, distinguishing between voluntarily returning stolen funds before detection and repaying them after being caught. The latter, he indicated, carried less weight in assessing accountability.

Lum addressed the court and apologised for his conduct, expressing regret for disappointing his family and community. The gallery appeared to consist largely of relatives, friends and supporters of Lum.

Contacted for comment following Monday's sentencing, a spokesperson for the Woodruff provided the following statement: "We respect the court’s decision and remain focused on fulfilling our mission, responsibly managing our resources and connecting deeply with the audience we serve."

Both the Woodruff and the High declined to provide substantive information about financial safeguards introduced following Lum's misconduct, or whether they intend to publish a remediation plan addressing financial controls, governance reforms and oversight procedures.

Toyota Confirms New GR Celica By Announcing 2027 World Rally Championship Car

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The heralded sports car nameplate will finally return as part of Toyota’s racing outfit—and then, presumably, on a production car too.

On Tuesday, Toyota officially confirmed that the Celica will return to the Japanese automaker’s family, but its first appearance seemingly won’t be as a production car. Instead, Toyota’s motorsport outfits—Toyota Racing and Gazoo Racing Toyota—will enter two models in the 2027 World Rally Championship ... and one will be a Toyota GR Celica.

The Celica's return has been rumored for years, but this is the first time Toyota has used the official name. Late last year, Cooper Ericksen, Toyota North America’s senior vice president of product planning and strategy, said a model that could be the Celica was in “pretty advanced development.” Now, Toyota says it will have a Celica WRC car ready by late January, when the Rally Monte Carlo will kick off the season.

Welsh rally driver and 2026 WRC title winner Elfyn Evans will head up Toyota Racing’s WRC effort alongside former Gazoo Racing driver Oliver Solberg. Solberg was the runner-up in the 2026 WRC season, narrowly missing the crown after a crash on the final power stage at the Rally Italia Sardegna. Finnish WRC driver Sami Pajari will also join the team.

“Before now, there was a specific regulation that said that for Rally1 homologation, there’s only one model allowed per manufacturer. That regulation has been changed to say, as long as the main mechanical components remain identical, you can do a variant model of your Rally1,” Tom Fowler, technical director of Toyota Gazoo Racing, said to DirtFish. “We had this opportunity to use this as a marketing tool and as a tool to increase the participation and to make things more interesting. There’s not really a big change; in the past we have run four, five or six cars on an event—but they’ve all been the same. This time, instead of running identical cars, we’re going to run two cars.”

Mechanically, this means the current GR Yaris Rally1 and the incoming GR Celica Rally1 will be largely similar. 2027 WRC Rally1 cars will continue to use a spaceframe chassis with turbocharged 1.6-liter inline-four engines and homologated suspension, running gear, and brakes from the current Rally2 category.

Toyota has yet to provide any other details about the Celica rally car beyond its name, but homologation regulations for the 2027 WRC season require production of at least 10 units within 24 months of the homologation date. Additionally, Toyota must be capable of supplying at least 10 race-ready WRC27-type cars per calendar year to customers.

Both of those regulations pertain to rally-specific models, not road-going models. Hence, the announcement that a Celica is coming to WRC doesn’t necessarily confirm the imminent arrival of a road car. Toyota executives previously confirmed the Celica would likely have an internal combustion engine, but declined to provide specific details, and the Celica’s shape, power figures, drivetrain layout, and number of seats have yet to be announced.

The Celica has a long history in WRC, first competing in 1972. Toyota entered the Celica in WRC through the 1980s and 1990s, with Carlos Sainz Sr. taking the drivers' titles in 1990 and 1992. Juha Kankkunen and Didier Auriol continued the Celica winning streak in 1993 and 1994. Toyota’s factory-supported Celica WRC run ended after the automaker earned a year-long WRC ban in 1995 over an illegal turbo restrictor, though privateers continued racing the model later in the 1990s.

A New York transplant hailing from the Pacific Northwest, Emmet White has a passion for anything that goes: cars, bicycles, planes, and motorcycles. After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. The woes of alternate side parking have kept his fleet moderate, with a 2014 Volkswagen Jetta GLI and a BMW 318i E30 street parked in his Queens community.

Delta Plans Another Austin Route

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Delta is expanding its Austin, Texas, network with a new daily route set to launch next spring.

The carrier will begin flights between Austin and Northwest Arkansas National Airport, which serves Fayetteville and the surrounding region, on March 7, 2027. SkyWest will operate the route under the Delta Connection brand using an Embraer E175.

Service from Northwest Arkansas to Austin begins March 8. Tickets are now on sale.

Flights will depart Austin at 8:59 p.m. and arrive in Northwest Arkansas at 10:49 p.m. The return flight will leave at 6 a.m. and arrive in Austin at 7:50 a.m. All times are local.

“Connecting Central Texas and Northwest Arkansas with new nonstop service brings two dynamic, growing regions closer together while giving our customers even more choice and convenience,” Luis Guitart, Delta’s Austin-based regional sales manager, said in a statement.

Guitart described the addition as another example of the airline’s investment in Austin.

Delta has been building its Austin operation, with plans to offer more than 70 peak-day departures to approximately 30 destinations by summer 2027. That expansion includes new service to Paris and San Diego, a second daily flight to San Jose, California, and year-round service to Cancun, Mexico.

First Lite Uncompahgre Foundry Jacket, Tested and Reviewed

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 3, 2026 4:36 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

It’s not often that you get to hunt in an area that is the namesake for the jacket you’re wearing, but this fall, I did just that. The Uncompahgre National Forest covers almost a million acres of high-elevation backcountry in Colorado. At, and over, 10,000 feet, the wind and weather can be unpredictable. So your clothing needs to be versatile and ready for whatever Mother Nature might throw your way.

After wearing the Uncompahgre Foundry Jacket during my first-rifle elk hunt with fluctuating temperatures, driving rain, and even a healthy dose of snowfall, I will be the first to tell you that this jacket earned its name. It’s surprisingly packable, offers zippered ventilation around the triceps, and is incredibly warm. Since my elk hunt, it’s earned a spot in my treestand layering system as well. Here’s everything you need to know about this must-have outer layer.

As I mentioned above, the weather on my elk hunt was unpredictable at best. From driving rain for multiple days on end, to rapidly dropping temperatures bringing snow into the high country, I got to experience the full spectrum of temperatures and conditions. The Uncompahgre Foundry jacket was one piece of my kit that I wouldn’t have wanted to live without.

It remained durable after hiking through oak brush, deadfall, and large conifer stands, but more importantly, it kept me dry. Since I was living in a tent, my biggest fear was getting wet, and thus, staying wet with no way to really dry off given the conditions. Thankfully, this jacket both figuratively and literally had my back.

Upon returning home, I tested the Uncompahgre Foundry jacket throughout an October cold front here in Pennsylvania as well. While the conditions were drastically improved compared to the backcountry in Colorado, this jacket still fit into my kit wonderfully. Rather than wear it the entire walk to the stand, I simply packed it up into a ball and tossed it in my pack. After climbing in, I’d throw it on and enjoy my sit. It keeps me warm and surprisingly doesn’t make a ton of noise when you’re moving to grab your bow and draw back.

While I do really like this jacket, there’s always room for improvement. The first thing I noticed was the pocket design and layout. On the front of the jacket are four pockets. Two magnetic dump pockets, and just above those, two zippered hand warmer pockets featuring a warm tricot lining. Both are quite handy for different things, but I often found myself digging around in the wrong pocket due to their layout and close proximity to each other. If it were up to me, I’d leave the dump pockets where they are, and move the hand warmer pockets to a more traditional, hip-centric location.

I also had an issue, that was certainly in part user error, where the hand warmer pockets got wet during a popup rain shower. I had accidentally left the zipper open, but due to the design, the rain ran down the front of the jacket and right into those pockets. When the zippers were closed, no water intrusion occurred. User error aside, I’d argue it’s another reason to relocate the hand warmer pockets.

Where the Uncompahgre Foundry jacket shines truly is in its versatility. I was able to wear it in temperatures ranging from 20 degrees, all the way up to 50 degrees. Admittedly, 50 degrees might have been right on the edge of too hot for this jacket, it really did a nice job of ventilating when I opened up the tricep vents.

It’s incredibly windproof, waterproof, durable, and warm. Honestly, it’s an outer layer I didn’t even know I needed until I had it. And now that I have it, it’ll be accompanying me on every hunt for the remainder of the fall and winter.

As an avid hunter, I came to the realization years ago that you get what you pay for when purchasing hunting clothing. I hate being cold, and I hate being wet. Thankfully, the Uncompahgre Foundry jacket does a wonderful job of eliminating both of those concerns. If you’re hunting for a new outer layer to get you through the rut, rifle, and late season, look no further.

Derek Horner is the manager of audience development for Outdoor Life where he manages OL’s social accounts and email campaigns, and shares our stories with the world. He was born and raised in central Pennsylvania where his father and grandfather taught him to hunt and fish. He graduated from Pennsylvania State University in 2018 and still lives in central Pennsylvania.
